ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e80	Former Major Lauritz Bernhard Sirevaag is dead, 90 years old. Lauritz B. Sirevaag was born on 14 March 1926 in Stavanger. He took the degree artium in 1947 and then trade education. From 1948 to 1965 he was head of department of Sandnæs Dampskips- Aktiesgesellschaft, and then transferred to A/S Norske Esso. Sirevaag was a member of Sandne's city government from 1963 to 1971. From 1967 to 1969 he was the rapporteur in Sandnes. He left the rapporteur's office when he was elected to the Parliament of the Right in Rogaland in 1969. Sirevaag spent eight years on the Stortinget and was a member of the Social Committee and the Industry Committee. In both committees he was a very active representative, but it was probably the business policy that was closest to his heart. He was a delegate to the UN General Assembly and was a deputy to the board of directors of the Stortinges Group of the IPU. After the years at Stortinget, Sirevaag was employed by the Rogalandsbank as bank manager. In 1990 he became Deputy Managing Director of the same place, and after the merger with Focus Bank he was the district bank manager of Rogaland until he left retirement in 1992. Sirevaag got a comeback in politics when, in 1987, he was elected to Rogaland County. Here he sat for the whole of 12 years – the first four years also as a member of the County Committee. Sirevaag had a number of public missions. He was, among other things, Chairman of the Business Assembly of Great Norske, member of the Executive Board of the District Development Fund and member of the Telewerk Council. He also had a number of boards in business and trust in sports. Lauritz B. Sirevaag was a socialist of rank and followed up both in politics and in society for the rest. He was an honorary member of the Rogaland Right, and it was well deserved that Lauritz B. Sirevaag in 1998 received the King's medal in gold in 1998. Lauritz B. Sirevaag was a politician with clear opinions, but he never rescued the chauffeur. He was always concerned about finding good solutions. This also contributed to his good mood and friendly association. We remember Lauritz B. Sirevaag for his work in the Storting, and shine peace over his memory.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e359	As the rapporteur of the matter, I would like to thank the entire committee for contributing to a good and informed case, where different views are presented in a transparent and straightforward manner. In particular, the Committee had a limited consultation from different legal environments which provided a good basis for the work of the Committee. Before I go into the various proposals in the case, it might be useful to have a little review of what the Stortinget has decided on various occasions related to the management of wolves in Norway. We can start with the treatment of St.meld. No 15 for 2003–2004, Rovvilt in Norwegian nature. This established the principle of own wolf zone with three annual juveniles. So this was a few years before we got the Natural Diversity Act, and that's why hunting for the wolf was allowed in the wild -- that it must «by means of its own regulations, the licence hunt for wolves shall be opened when there is a surplus in addition to the required stock target. The hunting of wolves may also be carried out in accordance with specified criteria within the management area if this is appropriate for regulating the stock.» In other words, the size was clear that we should have an active management of wolves in the same way as for the other predators. The reference to the place of residence in the wild was for the killing of the bear, the herd, the wolf and the gaupe in order to prevent damage to the buff and the tamrein. In 2009 we received the Nature Diversity Act under the Red-Green Government, and here the question was raised about how the Robbery Agreement from 2004 would be in relation to the new law. The Right Party and the Progressive Party wanted their own legislative wording which said that the administrative objective of the law would not apply to species for which there are other national administrative guidelines. The majority were satisfied with comments which concluded that the government's policy with regard to the largest predators is based on the predatory agreement and that decisions on the nature diversity law do not mean that the principles established there need to be changed. So in 2011, there was a new agreement that all the parties in the Stortinget were behind. The first point of the Decision is that Norwegian wild game management ‘shall take place within the framework of the provisions of the Nature Diversity Act and its treatment by the Storting, the Berne Convention and the two-part objective of the 2004 rapprochement and its follow-up’. The latest chapter of the history of the Great Britain Wolf Decision has been reconciled on the basis of Report. St. 21 for 2015–2016, Wolf in Norwegian nature. Behind this agreement stood the Workers' Party, Right, Advance Party and Christian People's Party with a large majority in the Stortinget. The majority once again states that the principles set out in the 2004 and 2011 agreement will form the basis for the further management of Norwegian wolf by nature and a new stock target will be set for four to six young people, including border revirons with a factor of 0,5, and the objective of three helenian juveniles will be maintained. Another thing that happens in 2016 is that for the first time when the population targets are set, we record seven whole-norse juveniles and four in border revir, a total of nine juveniles. So for the first time, the law on natural diversity and the question of licensing were tested because the wolf population was above the stock. Based on the new target of four to six young people, the predatory wildlife names within the zone are decided on the licensing of a total of four flocks. This will be the subject of an appeal, and the Department will repeal the decision, including: having regard to the fact that the nature diversity law does not provide sufficient justification because the damage potential of buff and tamrein was not considered to be large enough. The Council of State made a statement in Stortinget on 17 January. It is debated on 31 January, where the Stortinget asks for a case before 10 March – and here we are now. The Government's proposal is to amend the Nature Diversity Act by inserting the last indent of Article 9 of the Berne Convention into the Nature Diversity Act, which was not done in 2009. The provision states that, on the condition that there is no other satisfactory solution and that the withdrawal will not be harmful to the survival of the population concerned, the Parties may allow, in strictly controlled forms, on a selective basis and to a limited extent, the capture, storage and other discretionary use of certain wild animals and plants in a small number. The Government's proposals have not been accepted by the Committee for various reasons. For the government parties, it was impractical to change the law when one of our cooperation parties did not want to do this. We have, as we know, an agreement for parliamentary work where one of the points is that if the parties do not agree on anything else, the law of natural diversity will remain in place. I leave to the other parties to explain why they do not support the change in the law, but the majority behind the vote has stressed the flexibility that is felt within the law we have. Both the limited consultation of the committee and the different consultation input into the legislative proposal pointed out that there could be a basis for licensing in the natural diversity law § 18 first paragraph, point (c), which refers to general health and safety reasons or other public interests of major importance. The government has also opened up such a discussion in the proposition by writing: ‘However, the sum of private interests could overlap with public interests and what is initially a private interest may become a public interest if it affects a sufficient number. The consideration of the provision of industries such as out-landing and hunting may be relevant in this context. One might also imagine that reduced life expectancy due to the fear of wolf for a larger group of people and over time can be considered to affect public interests.» [[or other large predators]] This gripped the parties behind the 2016 agreement, including with the Central Party, and we put in a new concept, ‘district policy considerations’, as a possible basis of trust. The majority point to a negative impact on, for example: grazing, other industrial activities, hunting, the safety of the local population and psychosocial conditions of a general nature must be considered as factors which can fulfil the condition of territorial policy considerations. The same batches also propose that the predatory game regulations refer to the basis of the nature diversity law instead of the game law that was replaced in 2009. A claim for damages may be covered by Section 18 of the First paragraph of the Nature Diversity Act (b) and a licence application may be made in points (b) and (c) of the first subparagraph. The parties also have a proposal that the majority comments should be taken into account for the further management of the wolf in Norway. All parties, except the Centre Party, are behind a proposal to carry out a professional review of the Norwegian sub-population of wolves. There are also two minority proposals on the management plan and funding for municipalities with wolfevir, which I expect the parties behind to present themselves. So finally some words about solving the proposal that is notified here in the hall today. It may be appropriate to recall that the Nature Diversity Act was passed in 2009 under a different government, and that we now have to test the law against the stock targets for the first time. The majority comments and the majority proposals today seek to address the imbalance between the law and the stock targets set by the Storting. The answer to whether we succeed lies in the hands of this government, the government that comes after the election, and ultimately perhaps in the court. Few things create as steep fronts as how predators, and wolves in particular, should be managed. There's a hairy balance between different interests. All parties on the Stortinget have a responsibility to mitigate the level of conflict. In this case and in similar cases, the right has tried to find solutions that have the widest possible upswing in Stortinget, and I also feel that we have succeeded.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e403	Let me start by thanking the rapporteur for the work in Stortinget, who has been demanding because the committee has not found the basis for the position submitted for consideration in Stortinget, in the legislative proposition promoted by the Government of Stortinget. Let me also draw up a little bit of the story. When the Workers' Party joined a majority on wolf management in Norway in June last year – the so-called wolf settlement – there were two considerations that were absolutely crucial and bearer of the Workers' Party. First of all, we thought it was in place to put in place a government that dampened an increasing conflict between people and society on the one hand and the wolf on the other. Secondly, we would help to ensure the continuation of the two-part objective that has underpinned predatory policies and that through it we could have sustainable management, which ensured a reproductive wolf tribe. I mean – and it is not rejected in any way either – that the two purposes were very good in the position that Stortinget agreed to last June. In the debate held last June in Stortinget, there were no signals from the government or from the Council of State that would indicate that Stortinget’s decision could not be implemented. On the contrary, the State Council of Helgesen, in Stortinget, on 6 June, said: ‘I also record the fact that the majority of the Committee has made several appointments to the Government. It is important to have knowledge-based management, and the government should follow up these tasks. The Ministry of Climate and Environment will follow the changes adopted in the stock target and zone setting so that they can enter into force quickly. We will also inform the entire management of the predatory game of the changes and their consequences for the management." More loyal to the Storting setting, it can hardly be said to be. On that background, of course, the administration started. The regional predators and the statesmen set in motion an administration in line with what was said in the Stortinget. Then the predators came to their conclusion. The decision was appealed, and in December – almost half a year after the Storting had dealt with the matter – the government and the Council of State came with their summary of the complaint. It was then said that there was no licence for the collation of the wolf. But there is an assessment based on a limited part of the Natural Diversity Act, namely the Natural Diversity Act, § 18(b). That decision, of course, meant a massive response, both from the large majority who had assumed that the majority of the Storting was followed up, and not least from the population affected. The conflict escalated quite considerably. Then we ended up with the Council of State being forced to make a statement to the Stortinget, which was held on 17 January with a subsequent debate on 31 January, when the Council of State was very clearly instructed by the Stortinget to come back to Stortinget with a case and a handling that could correspond to what was the basis for the decision of the Stortinget in June 2016. The question immediately after the presentation by the Council of State and after the conclusion had been reached in December was why the provision of § 18(c) of the Law on Natural Diversity had not been used, where it states: «in order to take care of public health and safety interests or other essential public interests.» At the time, the Council of State announced that the preparatory work did not open up for this. But that the Council of State through the bill, which is now being dealt with in the Storting, has not provided the basis for opening up that understanding and the very clear interpretation, to me, is amazing. When the Committee began its work on this matter, all the legal input received by the Committee was very clear and clear that, in order to comply with the decision of the Stortinge of June 2016, it is the understanding and interpretation of Section 18(c) of the Natural Diversity Act that must be the basis. In the light of the clear legal advice given, the Committee has examined and interpreted what we consider to be the basis for understanding in connection with Section 18(c). The majority of the Committee considers very clearly that regional policy considerations must be regarded as a significant public interest, under the Nature Diversity Act § 18, first paragraph, point (c). The majority believe that if the wolf population has a negative impact on the rural policy objective of maintaining a widespread settlement in Norway, it may allow for derogations. The majority would also point out that a complex assessment of different factors needs to determine whether this condition is met. In the setting, we continue to say – to clarify it very clearly from Stortingen's pulpit: ‘The majority would stress that the assessment of whether the conditions covered by the concept of ‘public interest of material importance’ have been met must be carried out in each decision on killing. The majority would point out that licensing is only permitted where withdrawals do not threaten the survival of the stock, no other satisfactory solution has been found, and when the public interest that exists is considered to be essential. ‘... So there's an encroachment according to the law and what it's possible to open up to. The majority further believe and are very clear that such as hunting practice, both as a basis for food and outdoor activities, are important territorial policy considerations that can be given weight if an ever-growing predatory bird population affects deer populations to such an extent that the basis for the hunting exercise is demonstrated to be significantly impaired. So what we in the committee have done very clearly – I could refer to several aspects that the committee specifically refers to – is to say very clearly that Section 18(c) of the Natural Diversity Act is to use also in relation to the management of wolves, in line with what is the purpose of the agreement from June 2016. We also say very clearly that although wolves should have a stronger protection than outside the wolf zone, there should still be room for local nutrition, housing, general social activities and recreational and outdoor activities within the wolf zone, in the same way as in other districts. We even go so far in this majority that we stress that the majority behind the wolf settlement in Stortinget does not want the wolf zone to develop into a wolf reserve. Through three points of the proposal for a decision, and in particular in paragraphs 1 and 2, we are very clear on the expectations of practice. There is an instruction and a very clear clarification of what the government should do – point one is to change regulations. It is unnatural that the Storting points this out, but with the background of the debate and events of June last year, we believe this time that it is natural to do so. We also stress that the majority of comments in this position are to be used as a basis for further follow-up, practice and management of wolves in Norway. What is crucial to the outcome of this case and an assessment related to trust/untrust is, of course, how the government chooses to respond to the setting that has now been submitted. I have a expectation that the Council of State will clearly and crediblely establish that he intends to implement the majority position of the Storting to the point and point, and that perhaps the Prime Minister will confirm it as such. Everything else will be very unfortunate for the predatory game management in Norway. There is now a need to rebuild confidence in management and in the policy that should provide the basis for sustainable predatory strains in Norway.

But now, after a few years and a long time, the Center Party clearly also sees the need to change the criteria for withdrawals – which they themselves were involved in and introduced.

Norwegian wolf policy has an impact on people's lives, on how people live, on how areas are used for grazing, and on how people use nature. For some, the consequences are painful. When the Stortinge decided unanimously in 1986 to conclude Norway to the Berne Convention, it was stated from this pulpit that ‘Norway has a special responsibility to practise the Convention in accordance with its purpose. Norway took the initiative in the Convention and played an active role in making it best suited to the conservation of species resources in our and other countries’ flora and fauna.’ This reference from the then State Council Rachel Surlien reflects the Norwegian interest in the country's commitment to comply with international legal rules and that we ourselves bear such obligations. The Berne Convention provides clear guidance for predatory policies, which, for Norway, are not least reflected in the Nature Diversity Act. The general rule and the starting point under Norwegian law is that the killing of wolves is prohibited. So it was before the time of the Berne Convention. It was under Environment Minister Helga Gitmark in 1973 that the wolf was permanently saved in Norway – not without strong protests. ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e1379	I would like to present with this the presidency's position on the Storting on the issue of the downgrading of two documents in connection with the Storting's treatment of the Riksrevision's investigation into police and defence object protection. The main study report is exempted from publication for the sake of national security. There is a broad consensus that it is not appropriate to downgrade this. The documents on which this case is concerned are, firstly, the summary prepared by the National Audit Office of the main report for publication and, secondly, the letter from the Ministry of Defence giving detailed reasons why the Ministry considers that the summary cannot be published. These two documents are classified ‘confidential’ and ‘restricted’ respectively under the Security Act. The reason why the presidency places the issue of degradation before the Storting is that, in a letter dated 28 March of this year, the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee asks the Presidency to initiate a process for the Storting to downgrade those two documents. As further explained in the vote, the question of the degradation of the Riksrevisions summary has been presented to the Minister of Defence, the Minister of Justice and the Prime Minister in several rounds. The subsequent conclusion is that the government insists that the downgrading may harm the national security or other vital and national security interests and that the Grand Duchy’s request for downgrading cannot be met. If the Grand total is to degrade, if appropriate, an overall presidency shall assume that this must be done in the form of law. This is explained in more detail in the statement. On the other hand, on the question of whether the Storting should adopt such a law, the presidency is divided. A majority of the members of the Presidency, the members of the Right, the Advance Party and the Christian People's Party, are moving towards degradation, while a minority, the members of the Workers' Party, are promoting proposals for a special law on degradation. I will briefly explain the majority's position and assume that the minority will explain its position in a later article. The majority of the Presidency considers that, in exceptional situations, the Storting should only take advantage of its ability to downgrade classified documents. As an argument for downgrading, there has been evidence of the downgrading of the Lund Commission report in 1996. The majority believe that today's case on essential points deviates from the case at the time. First, the Lund Commission was set up by the Storting itself. Furthermore, the Commission’s theme — the examination of allegations of illegal monitoring of Norwegian citizens — was the subject of major public debate, the strong public interest involved, and it was mainly in the past — all this in contrast to today’s case. In addition, at the time, the government understood that the report was being uninstalled, which is not the case now. The majority believe that the Degrading Storting will establish a new practice that brings the issue of degradation into the ongoing political work of the Storting in a very unfortunate way. The majority will also refer here to the internationally recognised principle that it is the publisher of the information who has decided that it must be kept secret, which must also determine the issue of publication. It is in the nature of the case that it will be very difficult for the Storting in a qualified way to review the professional judgements of the administration on the need for grading. In this case, there is also reason to stress that the documents and other documents in question in this case are actually available to all representatives of the Storting. The government has also signaled throughout the proceedings that it is concerned that the Storting should receive adequate information and that the government has therefore been prepared to provide further information on the matter in a closed hearing. The size will thus have access to all the information available in this case. It is therefore my and the majority's position in the presidency that, on a general basis, the Storting should exercise a very high degree of restraint in degrading secret information on its own initiative. A new practice in this field can also reinforce confidence in the relationship between magnification and government and build barriers to future information exchange. This will also be very unfortunate from a democratic point of view. On this basis, the majority of the Presidency considers that, in line with the request of the Control and Constitutional Committee, the downgrading should not be complied with and the majority therefore recommends that the matter be annexed to the minutes.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e1400	I take the floor on behalf of the minority in the presidency to present the minority proposal, proposal No. 1st@item: inlistbox The members of the Presidency of the Workers' Party propose a separate special law to downgrade the Riksaudition summary of the police and defence object security investigation and to downgrade the Ministry of Defence's letter, which argues against the publication of the summary. This case is about Norway's security, about our vulnerability, and about what kind of preparedness we actually have. The bombing in the government quarter and the killings on Utøya in 2011 showed us that Norway as a society was not well enough prepared for a terrorist attack. In the minutes and hours that followed, perhaps the most striking thing about the lack of readiness was that the individual offices and resources did not find each other, that there was not a good enough cooperation between civil and military preparedness, that much did not work, and that buildings and objects were not well enough secured. We got the Gjørv Commission and the Special Committee in Stortinget, led by Knut Arild Hareide. Clear conclusions were drawn on the importance of coordination between the agencies and good cooperation between police and defence. The national audit concludes in the object protection case that important objects are not secured and that this could pose a risk to life and health. These include: on the lack of cooperation between defence and police. The National Audit Office believes that our emergency authorities will not be able to provide adequate protection for the central features of society in a state of crisis or terror. On this basis, we believe that a downgrading is necessary in order to provide the public with a broader understanding of the matter than the very brief review of the study provided by the Riksrevision Document 1 provides. In a democratic society, it is important that there should be an open debate on parliamentary control. Not only the members of the Grand Duchy but also the citizens of the country have a legitimate interest in knowledge of weaknesses in national security, not least because the background to this matter lies in the Gjørv Commission's report on 22 July terrorism. The report, which gave an undamaged picture of the emergency situation, was entirely public. It belongs in this context as well. We are therefore probably very surprised that, despite repeated appeals from the Storting and from a united presidency, the government – let me stress it – has not found, in any way, to be able to meet the Storting's wishes for publicity. And let me add: they've had many times and ways to change their mind. The Presidency and the Administration have been trying for several months to get both the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Justice and Defence to deflect the case. We have also addressed the Prime Minister. Of course, we agree with the Presidency's majority that there should be serious societal interests in order for the Storting to oversee the government's assessments of the need for security-grade information. However, we believe that there are precisely such burdensome reasons that apply in this case. The issue is about the ability of police and defence to protect objects of fundamental importance to social security. The National Audit Office has used the term ‘very serious’ in its criticism. This is a form of criticism that is used only exceptionally, for example: if there is a risk to life and health. This suggests that the public, and not just wholesale representatives, should have insight into the matter. We have also placed considerable emphasis on the professional assessment of the National Audit Office in our recommendation. The National Audit Office considers that the summary can be published and that neither individual information nor manufacture as a whole violates the provisions of the Security Act. The intention to have a genuine public debate on the central aspects of the country’s preparedness concerns, we believe that the Storting should adopt a separate special law so that further information on the matter can be published. Finally, for the sake of order, let me say that it's not a government document that we're proposing that the Storting downgrade. It is a document from Stortinget's own control body, the Riksrevisionen. With this, I will take up the minority proposal in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e1515	Having referred to the previous article, I would like to say that there was a great deal to be done here, for those who listened carefully to that post will find that this is inscribed in a systematic series of inconsistency with regard to constitutional rules, for which unfortunately the Workers' Party's fraction in the control committee of all parts of the Stortinget has become a spokesman for. It is, of course, taken a little after memory, but in the previous article it was said something like this: The Storting has a responsibility for keeping the laws as determined by the legislator. With that statement, you've become a spokesman for a completely new state. This is based on the following: Storting, when they adopt a law, e.g. the Security Act, to which we are to return, provides it with the law for the purposes of follow-up, law enforcement and enforcement in the departments and, where appropriate, in the courts. Those who would disagree with the department's assessment of the law can always use the courts. So it's not the Stortinge that can go in and take over that legal application, and the whole reasoning of the representative Kolberg is based on the fact that we can go into the legal application of matters that interest us in particular. Here is the party policy that is the leader. In other respects, it has been shown that exemptions from the general rule can be made if there are overriding social interests. However, as defined in today's debate, when the opposition, led by the Workers' Party, wants to suspect the motives of the government precisely when it does what the Stortinge provided for, namely to comply with the Security Act. And then we have to get into the Security Act, because it instigates two very important principles that are also in line with international legislation in this area. It is that in a complex society with cross- and cross-border intelligence activities and with wide scope for spying, any public body involved in secret information should think through and take responsibility for proper grading, if necessary, for emergency and defence reasons. It establishes the Safety Act, while also the competent body, as the issuer of information, is responsible for assessing whether other matters, such as: Social debate or the culture of openness, which we also want to have in a democracy, want to downgrade. But there is no random fraction in the control committee, there is no Riksrevision assigned to any task, as was claimed by the representative Nybakk. The National Audit Office was almost given a sheriff's authority on behalf of the Stortinget. No, in this context, the Swedish National Audit Office is not aware of the safety law, and at least not of the role that representatives Kolberg and Nybakk will put in the Riksrevision. If you read through their post afterwards, you will see that they argue that it is because the Riksrevision has a different opinion – as an outsider, an instance that does not see the link between the hazards of information that can relate to publicly available information. There's something no one can assess from the outside. That's why one-sided responsibility for this has been placed on those who are issuing the information. It is this incorporation of party policy into concrete assessments of the documents that the majority of the Committee of Control has wanted to instigate and impose on the government. Fortunately, there is a minority in the presidency, and – more importantly – it appears fortunately that such an important element of the Security Act still has a majority in this Chamber. The majority of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs will then do – a committee which has always had full access to the secret parts of the reports, which on that basis can exercise that criticism and which can even address the issues, but not to the same level of detail when it comes to this matter later, in addition to all the possibilities they have for carrying out that control – is to turn to case handlers by itself taking over the exercise of judgement and depriving the competent safety authorities, which under law are entrusted with the task of assessing this, this exercise of judgement and incorporating it into their political judgement. After all, it was just to hear the committee chairman's strong emphasis on something that is not comparable, namely the Donor Commission's position, as far as I have understood, is not part of the security system. The Workers' Party's fraction expresses itself as if the issue of grading should be justified by a desire to spare the Defense and the police from criticism. There are just two open hearings, i.e. two days of consultation in full transparency – and probably a closed hearing, as a result of the debate we have today – which means that this matter has not yet been concluded in the control committee. With regard to the committee procedure, it would also have to be said that it would have been fine if the chairman of the committee sometimes remembered that there was disagreement in the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, because already during the discussion we had with the Presidency, and in the dialogue with the Council of State, the members of the Right and the Progressive Party had to draw up a very strong protocol on which, according to the law, the body is to decide on the grading and, if necessary, the downgrading of these documents. The members of the Right and the Progressive Party have always considered the letter of the majority to the Presidency of 14 March as an order to amend or extend the legal basis for downgrading documents. So it would simply be a practice that is contrary to the security law that we have. As stated in Section 11: ‘Any person who issues or otherwise makes displayable information shall ensure that the information is marked with the appropriate level of security.’;» Otherwise, I refer to the review instead. This is an obligation underpinned by criminal law in the same law, namely: ‘Any person gaining access to security-graded information as part of a work, assignment or duties of an establishment shall be obliged to prevent unauthorised persons from becoming aware of the information.’;» Thus, ‘irresponsible’ are those who are not authorised to carry out these assessments. So you would have broken the law if you had pressed the Riksrevision, which in this context really says that it's up to the Storting to do what they want with the case. The national audit has at least been aware that they could not downgrade, because they do not have legal competence to do so. However, I have to say here in this room that there has been a leak to the press that at the same time represents a violation of the criminal code in Section 31 of the Security Act that I referred to, and for more serious cases also the penal code. It should also worry the Storting. Since we are many concerned, middle-aged people participating in this debate, we should also be concerned about that part. For it is essential that a breach of obligations can occur — almost without anyone’s involvement — at some point in the course of the processing of a security-graded document, thereby suspecting all the bodies involved, whether in the Stortinget, individual representatives, or the Riksrevision or the two departments concerned. Unfortunately, there is an even greater potential for damage in this because the integrity of the size and administration depends simply on our ability to protect defence-critical information and preparedness-vital information, and that individuals do not go beyond those assessments in order to achieve their own political purposes or what it should be. The Labour Party's position on the matter, which has been further promoted almost in principle by the representative of Kolberg, provides the basis for wondering whether the Workers' Party at all takes into account the constitutional division of functions when dealing with issues of this nature, or whether everything that comes from there is currently marked by more opportunistic considerations, where one is willing to weaken the rule of law's power distribution, i.e. the interaction between the executive government, the ministry and the magnificence, and that one ignores these very important considerations that I have been faced with, precisely because, for more political and short-term reasons, one wants to criticise a government with which one is politically disagreeable. The Workers' Party wants the Stortinget to so deviate from the incorporated division of labour between legislative and executive powers by means of a special law, thus creating a dangerous precedent, if it is followed in the future, namely that the Stortinget may take over the proceedings itself after a committee has been set up, and may itself downgrade individual documents at its own discretion. It is not uncommon for the Stortinget and the Stortinget bodies to deal with security-grade information, and in 1999 the Rules of Procedure of the Stortinget were amended and extended to clarify the rules governing the management of classified information by the Stortinget, cf. Paragraph 75 of the Rules of Procedure. There are therefore procedures for both meetings, hearings and record-keeping so that classified information can be properly processed. I would also like to point out that this has recently been well taken care of by the Storting in dealing with the audits of the operational capability of the Home Office and the operational capability of the frigates. For the latter, an up-to-date summary was also prepared, in close cooperation with the National Audit Office. This consideration is also very well taken into account in the annual rated operational capability assessments provided to the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee. In my correspondence with Stortinget in this case, I have mentioned several times that safety-rated information must be handled in line with the legislation. In our parliamentary system, therefore, it has been arranged for the Storting to be properly informed about and dealt with matters of this nature, and these are the procedures followed by the government. It is very important that the Storting should exercise parliamentary control and that, as a general rule, it should be transparent. In this case, the Stortinget has conducted open consultations, but it is also important that the Stortinget has mechanisms for the closed and confidential treatment of classified information. This is an integral part of representative democracy, where representatives exercise control on behalf of the population also in matters which, by their very nature, cannot be fully published. It's in the interests of both the Biggest and the Government, because it allows thorough processing of this information. It is also important for the government to be able to fulfil its information obligation under the Constitution § 82 to provide necessary and comprehensive information to the Stortinget. The national audit was met by both the justice and defence sectors with full transparency, and they were also given full access to both forces and weaknesses. Since the material elements of the information were collateralised, it was also a prerequisite for its subsequent treatment. The public has been informed of the Riksrevision's criticism, and it should be lacking. This has been done through the unupgraded review of the audit in the Riksrevision Master File and through the public presentation of the case by the Riksrevision. On 20 and 21 March of this year, two open hearings were held in Stortingen, which also provided the public with a good understanding of the matter. Both the government, including the Prime Minister, responsible officials and others were represented in the hearing to answer Storting’s questions. The criticism of the National Audit Office is also duly covered in the media. Since the report was submitted by the National Audit Office last October, the matter has been discussed 600 times in editorial notices, debates and comments. In March of this year, this case constitutes 15 p.m. Of all the mentions of the Defense, and there's a lot. In this context, it may seem strange that the minority’s decisive argument for overcoming the professional assessment and grading is ‘the intention to have a real public debate on key societal issues’. It is therefore well known that the Riksrevision has pointed out weaknesses in certain parts of the object protection preparedness for 2015. This is not a government that can or does not want to put a lid on it. The object security of the police and defence consists in securing important properties, areas, buildings, installations, means of transport and materials against security threats. In its 2017 threat and risk assessments, the police security service, the intelligence service and the national safety authority state that Norway and Norwegian interests are exposed to foreign intelligence activities that may have a high potential for damage. Information on civil and military infrastructure is collected through intelligence activities. In a tense situation or under a threat of terrorism, threats and estimates of important infrastructure can be used to exert pressure and create chaos. I would also like to remind you here, as I did in the hearing, of the quite significant change in the security policy situation in Norway and other countries. I therefore entirely disagree with what Mr Lundtigen says, that involving the question of espionage is ‘to shoot much, much more than a target’. On the contrary, it is at the heart of the assessments and assessment themes of the safety authorities. The intelligence interest in key Norwegian objects also includes getting as precise information as possible about any weaknesses in police and defence work in securing the objects. This applies, for example: information on the existence of weaknesses in work processes, operational capability, information flows, planning, competence measures and security measures. Compromising information that provides transparency in these circumstances could form part of the basis for future espionage and, in the worst cases, also in the planning of sabotage, terror and armed attack. In other words, it is important not to point out the area where potential threat actors can search for information relevant to their desire to harm Norwegian interests. There are threat actors with significant resources, significant competences and ability and willingness to challenge and harm Norwegian interests. The Presidency clearly states in its setting that it is the issuer of the information that has the competence to assess the rating issue. So in this context, it's the Ministry of Defence, not the National Audit Office, or anyone else, who's the issuer of the information. I'm saying it that way, because it's the Riksrevision that gave the reports, but it's based on information issued by the ministry, and there's no doubt that it's the department that's the issuer of the information. That competence also covers the question of downgrading or downgrading. It follows from law and regulation, in addition to internationally recognised principles, and is based on what is a reasonable and natural division of functions between state powers. It is natural that the person who issues the information and who has intelligence, information on how potential threat actors operate, and what information they are seeking, are closest to assessing the potential for damage to the information to be compromised. I also think it would be tempting to ask the special representative, Mr Lundteg, who took it straight up. He says that ‘there is no factual basis for grading under the Safety Act, from our point of view’. I am curious about the professional basis on which the representative Lundtigen builds that assumption. I also note that there is a consensus in the presidency that there must be particularly serious social considerations if the Stortinget is to degrade information by law in contravention of what the competent authority considers advisable. Security-grade information is, of course, vague precisely because of public interests — not increasing potential vulnerabilities in society. In this particular case, the Stortinget has had full access to all information as a basis for carrying out its control task, and the public is familiar with the main findings and criticisms of the Riksrevision's unrated publicity and public presentation of the case, the Storting's open hearings and a wide-ranging debate and discussion of the matter in the media. It is important to bear in mind that the Safety Act speaks of both object safety and information security, protecting information that, if it comes out or compromised, may cause harm. The Ministry of Defence, as the issuer of the information contained in the relevant summary of the National Audit Office, is required under the Security Act to rate information that needs to be protected for security reasons. This is not something we can choose to ignore. The Ministry of Defence has dealt with the grading of the case documents in this case in the same way as other cases and made a thorough professional assessment of the damage potential of the information. The fact that a matter is given attention in the media, as it should and should be given, cannot be a reason to disregard the considerations of information security imposed on us by the Security Act. It is the opinion of the Ministry of Defence that the National Audit Office’s proposal for an additional unrated summary in sum contains information which, if compromised, could in some way lead to harm to the security of the State or other vital national security interests. Therefore, the information has to be graduated. The legal conditions for the security of information are that safety reasons make this necessary. In the assessment of the safety rating, the content of the document as a whole must also be considered. The total amount of information contained in the document in question constitutes a knowledge base of protection under the Security Act, because it relates collectively to the national security and vital national security interests. An attempt has been made to create the impression that the government has graduated the National Audit Office’s proposal for a further graduated summary in order to protect itself from criticism or to avoid criticism. I want to strongly refute this. The government sees the Riksrevision's findings as serious, important and necessary, something that both the Minister of Justice, the Prime Minister and I were ready for in the hearings in March. We want a public debate on security and preparedness, and I believe that we have had it over the period from October to now. At the same time, we must ensure that security and preparedness do not suffer harm by disregarding the provisions of the Security Act on classified information. Norwegian preparedness for terrorism and war is an area where much information needs to be security-graded, and therefore there can be no full transparency in this area. I also feel that there is an understanding in Stortinget, through the posts that have been held here. Criticism is also evident in documents where the level of detail of the security-graded information is smaller. There has been no lack of criticism, despite the fact that some information has been graded for the sake of national security. As far as the Ministry's draft of a possible summary to the National Audit Office is concerned, the Ministry handled it in the same way as when we had a cooperation with the National Audit Office on the Frigate Report, a cooperation that resulted in an unrated summary, with which I think all parties were well pleased. I would also strongly reject the fact that this should be censorship. This was an attempt to provide an additional classified summary but did not contain the security-graded details to be protected under the Security Act. ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e2024	This issue concerns a topic that most people are familiar with during many years of debate in the public sector and in the United Kingdom. In the last year, the debate on the impact assessment of areas outside the Lofoten, the Vestralen and Senja has again been on the agenda. Because of this, the positions of the different batches are already known. The issue we are discussing today is a proposal from the Left, and there is one proposal on the matter. The government is asked to propose permanent protection against petroleum activity in the sea areas outside the Lofoten, the West Sea and the Senja, including the whole of Northland VI, Northland VII and Troms II. This proposal does not get a majority in the Chamber today, but although opinions are shared on the need for an impact assessment of precisely these marine areas among the members of the Committee, it is a large majority of members who support some important principles for the management of our common petroleum resources. I'm glad. The majority of the Committee points to strict health, environmental and safety requirements for all petroleum activities and that this is one of the main carriers of Norwegian petroleum policy. At the same time, the main policy objective is to facilitate profitable oil and gas production in the long term. Oil and gas industries are still Norway’s most important industry, despite the fact that the last few years have been tough due to low oil prices and that we are in the process of making a green shift. It's going to be most important for years to come. The majority of the Committee considers that good framework conditions, including access to new areas of Norwegian soil, are essential for the ability of the oil and gas industry to maintain its value creation, secure existing and create new jobs in the future. There is broad support in the Stortinget for continued activity on Norwegian pedestal, also in new areas. As I said at the outset, there are shared opinions in Norwegian policy on activities in the sea areas outside the Lofoten, the Vesterålen and Senja. These are valuable areas for Norway, both nature and environment, fish and petroleum resources. Whether for impact assessment or for lasting protection, there is no doubt that there is a common political objective that all exploitation of natural resources in Norway should be carried out in a sustainable and safe manner. The main difference in this case is whether or not one already knows enough to say no to petroleum activity in these specific areas. It is through the Petroleum Act and including impact assessment and opening processes that new areas of activity on Norwegian base are opened and regulated. When carrying out an impact assessment, there is a good basis for decision-making on how to proceed with an opening process. At the same time, there is an open and democratic process that ensures that all interests are heard. No impact assessment has been carried out for the marine areas mentioned in this proposal, with the exception of parts of Northern Ireland VI. Therefore, as the majority of the Committee sees, at present there is not a sufficient basis for decision on petroleum activities in these areas or for permanent protection. The primary position of the right in this case is clear. We want a full impact assessment of both Nordland VI, Nordland VII and Troms II. Only in this way will we be able to gain sufficient knowledge of both the opportunities and the challenges that lie in these areas. Potentially, there are very large values for Norway, not only financially but also through jobs and value creation. The right does not have the tradition of saying no to knowledge, and neither do we want to do that with the Lofoten, the West Pole and Senja. A yes to impact assessment is not an automatic yes to opening, but it will provide valuable knowledge that can form the basis for a good decision. In my view, an impact assessment is also important because we have a common responsibility to manage our petroleum resources in a good way, not only for us, but also for future generations. At the same time, during this great period, we have had an agreement with our cooperation parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left, not to carry out an impact study in the current period of mass destruction, and I respect that. But I also choose to see this representative proposal in the context that we are now on our way into an election campaign, and then I feel it right to give to know the Right's primary view on this issue.

We've got a Paris agreement, where we're not just going to aim to avoid global warming beyond 2 degrees, but we're going to reach 1.5 degrees. In a situation where we know that there are large oil resources that need to be left in the ground, there really is no need to go into the most vulnerable areas and start developing there. Nordic countries expect an export increase — based on what has been in the past — of 4.3 pst. this year. In particular, seafood products are the starting point for this growth. ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e2155	The main objective of Norwegian petroleum policy is to facilitate the profitable production of oil and gas in a long-term perspective. The role of a petroleum producer shall be combined with leadership also on environmental and climate issues. We've been both good and lucky in Norway. We have had a broad political majority that has supported petroleum policy for decades and ensured responsible management of our natural resources. It has served us well, today we have an oil and gas industry that is Norway's second largest and most important. Activities provide profitable jobs throughout the country. It has contributed to both industrial development, technology development, societal development and funding for our welfare development. The petroleum activities we have on Norwegian soil are carried out under strict health, environmental and safety requirements and the preservation of the external environment. No petroleum activity on the Norwegian base is carried out unless it can be carried out in a proper manner. Consent by authority and authority is required for all activities including search drilling, expansion, operational phase and closure phase. Co-existence with other users of the sea has always been a major concern. In areas with particularly vulnerable or valuable environmental values, additional strict business-related conditions may be imposed, e.g.: time limits in the case of seismic surveys and exploration in oil-bearing layers. The purpose of this type of condition is to limit the potential for environmental damage and to ensure co-existence with other sea-based industries. Let there be no doubt that the government is also concerned with safeguarding the fishing interests of the sea areas outside the Lofoten, the Vestral and Senja. In Norwegian resource management, sustainable use of marine resources is central. All Norwegian experience suggests that it is fully possible both to have a responsible resource activity and to provide important environmental and nature considerations. These experiences are a knowledge-based management tradition that we have every reason to be proud of. We have succeeded in building up several world-leading marine industries based on this knowledge-based management approach. There is a broad majority, including a broad majority in the vote on today's case, which joins in the continuation of this management tradition. The areas mentioned in this case, outside the Lofoten, the Vestral and Senja, are, as has been said, essentially not open to petroleum activities, with the exception of parts of Northern Ireland VI. Nor will new areas be opened for petroleum activities on Norwegian soil until an opening process under the Petroleum Act has been carried out. Such an opening process ensures both a sound decision-making basis and a broad involvement of all interested and interested parties. What is currently available from knowledge related to the areas outside the Lofoten, the Vestral and Senja areas is quite extensive, but in some cases not sufficient to implement the possible opening of these areas. Therefore, from a resource management point of view and the administrative tradition we have had, it is important to carry out a full impact assessment of the area before making any considerations as to whether to open the area or not, and whether to set specific requirements for any petroleum activity in these areas. There is an equivalent process running on any area of the cap to be assessed for a possible petroleum activity through an opening process. I would strongly warn against adopting such a proposal as the representatives initially propose in this proposal for a representative, and as a minority in the vote. We need to continue the rooted Norwegian administrative tradition of making decisions based on facts and knowledge. Something else would not be proper.

While talking a lot about international CO 2 prices and international agreements, and it is true that Norwegian oil production does not go into any agreed political accounts for Norway, international climate work has increasingly deviated from overall joint agreements and is increasingly governed by regional and bilateral agreements and obligations on national boundaries. So the need for Norway to increasingly take responsibility for its own oil production and its consequences is evident in the continuation of the Paris Agreement. It is interesting to see this in connection with the government's own proposal for a climate law, which we will discuss later, and which proposes that Norway cut between 80 and 95 pst. of our greenhouse gas emissions by 2050. Should we do it at the same time as we're a massive oil producer? Very strange. We are now in a situation where the Storting does not have much understanding of the Greens' proposal, but this is a proposal that we are going to come back to on the Stortinget. Starting investigation and planning of the closure of the Norwegian oil and gas industry is a matter for business policy, it is economically sensible, and it is above all to take our national responsibility for our impact on the climate of the earth and on the future of our children more seriously than what we are doing now, where we claim to be leading the climate, and produce more climate change per capita than all, most of the countries in the world.

It would be equivalent to endangering the competence base that petroleum activities have contributed to this country’s building over 50 years. The competence base currently associated with oil and gas activities in Norway may be one of Norway's most important contributors to solving the world's future energy and climate challenges. It ensures that we safeguard this prosperity, this environment of competence and this opportunity to further develop Norway for a further 50 years.

The EEA Agreement must be preserved and continued, and the Right is the guarantor of it. Norway must invest more, double aid for democracy, women, human rights and the free press. Developments in Turkey are worrying. Turkey after Atatürk has been in many ways an economic miracle. It is the common policy on asylum and immigration that is the trigger for the current discontent. Europeans most feel powerless and let their frustration go beyond the established parties. We must be careful to judge any country that is skeptical of the European Union. There is a tendency to say that countries cannot survive on their own, and that it leads to everything from Nazism and racism to what is worse. But it is true that for most of history countries have been independent countries, and they have also become independent countries without necessarily having to go to war against each other, so there are countries that can survive without being part of a Union, and Norway is a good example of that. The EU-Turkey relationship reinforces this picture. The EU requires Turkey to move in a more democratic direction before membership can become a reality. Turkey, for its part, claims that such conditions are a barrier to EU integration of a large Muslim country into the Union. Both parties may have something right in their assessments, but Turkey is holding a very strong hand through its control of a potential migration flow to Europe. Norway is a small and open country and is totally dependent on being able to sell goods to EU countries under the same conditions as EU countries have internally. Unlike larger countries such as the United Kingdom and France, it is not possible for Norway to act as an island with special trading conditions. This does not mean freeing and competition in all sectors, but it will mean that Norway will have to focus on the production of goods and services that we have good prerequisites for delivering cheaper or better quality than others. At the same time, it is important to stress that Norway does not export or import into and from the EU. We export and import to and from countries that are members of the EU. The EU is not a trading entity, but countries that have agreed to trade on a level playing field and that have established a level playing field for trade. Countries have established harmonised standards which impose obligations. There are technical requirements, safety requirements are either for the size of sockets, the socket on bulbs, the haze on the windows or the size of the keyholes. This may sound de minimis, but it allows for real competition between different suppliers, and it is crucial. All of this is positive. However, the challenge with the EU is not to create a level playing field for business. The challenge is to try to create a political union – that's where the challenge comes from. Countries have not wanted to listen to their own citizens and have ignored the national state in favour of European integration. Fortunately, people protest. People don't want to let go of their national identity. They want to retain their national characteristics; they want to maintain their national culture. This is exactly where the EU is bombing. The system itself has become self-enhancing. For what will the many thousands of EU employees be doing if they are not looking for new areas and the EU can replace national self-government? That's actually what they're set to do. They are not only working for a level playing field, as was the intention of the establishment of the European Joint Union, but they are looking for an overall political community, which may not exist and which may not exist. Trade in goods and services across borders has served all EEA and EU countries well. The main challenge has been the free movement of labour in recent years. This is due to different levels of well-being, different working conditions, different levels of pay, different social security benefits and not least different cultures. It has created challenges that have not been adequately addressed. Another consequence is that national labour forces gradually disappeared in some fields as imports of labour disappeared. The various social security systems of the EEA countries make the exchange of labour particularly problematic. For Norway, social security exports are something that can in the long term kick legs under the EEA Agreement, which was also addressed by the Council of State. It is very unreasonable and gives unfortunate incentives that, for example, if the recipient is resident in another EEA country, it is not possible to adjust social security benefits by purchasing power. For example, child welfare and cash support constitute 15.8 pst. of an average annual salary in Norway, but it amounts to 95,8 pst. of an average annual salary in Poland and 122.3 pst. of an average annual salary in Lithuania. The government has taken several measures to limit social security exports. This applies, inter alia, to: in the case of longer stays abroad and requirements for a residence prior to the exercise of the right to such different benefits. However, the issue shows that it is not unproblematic for the EU to include more and more countries, including countries where the level of pay and well-being is significantly lower. And then we have the flow of migration to Europe, an area where the EU has failed fundamentally. In its inability to understand — or, to put it correctly, to fail to understand — the EU’s leadership and the Member States have risen above their own citizens. In its desperate need to act politically correctly, the values on which the EU is based have been undermined. The Schengen Agreement and Dublin cooperation are under pressure and, moreover, are not a sustainable agreement for the future. In the short term, external border controls reduce the pressure on migration, but there are other factors that contribute to the opposite – not least the assistance and ferry traffic of European countries to carry migrants across the Mediterranean. This traffic should stop, it cannot continue, because there is no end to it if we continue along the same route – not necessarily that Norway should do it on its own, but we need cooperation, so that we end this and do not transport thousands of migrants every day from Africa to Europe. The protests we experience over large parts of Europe are positive and well-founded. The flow of migration from Africa and Asia has no end, and the continuation of today’s policies can have consequences far beyond what can be addressed today. It is therefore important for the EU to shift its immigration policy to the benefit of its citizens. The alternative I don't even dare to think about.

Producers and individual EU Member States have sometimes criticised Norway for the increases in customs duties, among others. meat and cheese, which came under the Stoltenberg II regime, in January 2013. In order to liberalise trade in agricultural products, Article 19, it is understandable that the EU requires access to the market. For Norway, the outcome of the negotiations implies the establishment of duty-free allowances, or total duty-free allowances, for the export of some Norwegian products. Such duty-free allowances will give Norwegian industry better access to the EU market. In addition, there has been a limited increase in existing import quotas for some of the EU's products. Overall, the Norwegian import quota for meat is increasing, for example: from the EU, with 2,550 tonnes and below the quota of 3100 tonnes entered into under the previous government, Stoltenberg II. All in all, it is a good result, which is well anchored in our good and forward-looking agricultural policy.

Norway had to negotiate bilateral trade agreements, tariffs and conditions on its own, including non-tariff barriers to third countries, i.e. individual agreements with the United States, Canada, China, etc. Norway would stand alone in such a process of negotiation and could not benefit from the EU’s negotiating power and negotiating capacity. In addition, this movement of goods, services, investment and labour must be disregarded. There are also other aspects, as the previous speaker pointed out, namely our participation in research and education policy, institutional cooperation and competition policy. Above all, since we now have the presidency of the Nordic Council of Ministers, it must also remind us that this would lead to the fragmentation and isolation of Norway from our Nordic neighbours, because the border between the EU and the EEA region and Norway would separate us from our Nordic neighbouring countries.

Norway’s participation in EU Horizon 2020 is the clear most important international research policy cooperation for Norway, not only for Norwegian researchers’ access to research environments in EU countries, but also as an important door opener for Norwegian researchers to countries outside the European Union. It gives us access to knowledge and improved quality through competition in an international arena. Horizon 2020 has three main objectives. This is a clear strengthening of today's income targets, i.e. farmers must have more in income growth in crowns than in other groups, because that is the only way to reduce the revenue gap. The Committee also notes that income is the main instrument for achieving the objectives of agriculture. Secondly, the Committee will facilitate investment and strengthen the economy for all types of use. The Committee believes that investment firms should also be targeted at small and medium-sized applications. The Committee draws particular attention to the need for investment in milk by between 15 and 30 cows. In addition, the majority of the parties to the agricultural negotiations are asking for clarification of the question of introducing an operational difficulty. Thirdly, a single committee of the government is asked to put forward a fund scheme in agriculture. Fourthly, it is gratifying that a joint committee opposes the imposition of a levy on the biological processes in agriculture, as proposed by the Green Tax Commission. Biff tax is not good climate policy. Fifthly, the majority call for a better follow-up of the government's soil protection strategy. The management of the land resources shall, in the Committee's view, have an eternity perspective. Of course, the center part is not 100% satisfied with this setting. The self-sufficiency of food needs to be strengthened. Norwegian food production should also secure Norwegian consumers environmentally friendly and safe food. The value chain for food is our largest continental industry, a green value chain that ensures income for the country and work for people on the garden, in the food industry and at the trade. If we are to manage to increase Norwegian food production, we must have farmers throughout the country, and we must use the agricultural area throughout the country. Where food is grown, it will feed, and where there is a plough, there will be ploughs. The government's struggle to liberalise and centralize agriculture is not viable. ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e3865	Thank you to the Committee for a good and long-term work on this matter. Special thanks also to the Christian People's Party and the Left for good and constructive negotiations and results. The main objective of agricultural policy shall be to increase food production. We want to facilitate the good development of the agricultural industry for small and large uses across the country, with food security, agriculture across the country, increased value creation and a sustainable low-carbon agriculture as the overall objective. A Joint Committee takes the view that farmers, as self-employed persons, should be able to enjoy the same incomes as others in society. Income opportunities must be available within a varied use structure in order to allow Norwegian agriculture to exploit its production potential, to ensure recruitment and to increase its potential for income in the industry. An overall committee believes that the income target should be to reduce the income gap between agriculture and other social groups. Good market adaptation and productivity development will be a prerequisite for the formation of income. Agricultural policy must be designed to strengthen the competitiveness of Norwegian food producers. Norwegian agriculture faces competition for labour and capital from other industries and from non-resident food producers. It is therefore essential that agricultural policy enables farmers to meet these developments. For several decades, Norwegian farmers have been at the end of their productivity in Norway. This has led to the production of as much or more units as before. The number and number of farmers have therefore fallen for many decades. Now, however, the decline has flattened somewhat, and there are fewer farmers leaving the industry. This development is joyful, and there are a number of positive signs that Norwegian agriculture is progressing well for the time being. Figures from NIBIO show a good growth in income, although it varies from part of the country to part of the country and between productions. Food production is increasing, less use and less land is out of production, new cultivation is high, more young rates, and there is a high demand for investment funds. In 2016, it was 20 pst. more highly qualified in agriculture as the first choice in higher education than it was in 2015. The proportion of Norwegian raw materials in the feed is increasing. Appropriations for forest roads and lumberland have increased by from 80 to 100 pst. during this period. And former Minister of Agriculture Trygve Slagsvold Vedum's words: ‘There will be no Norwegian cabbage with Erna’, has been thoroughly shamed when we now have a market balance and more of it on lamb. The government has led an agricultural policy where stimulating increased food production and efficient farming has been important. Changes in grant rates have been made and simplifications have been implemented in the grant system. Some ceilings have been removed in support and quota schemes in order to facilitate the exploitation and investment of new technologies and take advantage of economies of scale where these assumptions exist. This policy has resulted in farmers producing more food in recent years and having a good income growth. We see young people bet in the industry. It gives optimism and faith in the future for the industry. Although changes in grant rates and quota schemes have been made, farmers who operate on a smaller scale still receive significantly more support per animal or per deer than those who operate on a larger scale. Geographical and structural differences are maintained through rural grants and freight subsidies to ensure the basis for agriculture throughout the country. There is no contrast between stimulating economies of scale where possible and stimulating agriculture throughout the country. By aligning the instruments so that e.g.: The use of grazing resources can both be achieved for the part of agriculture that can exploit these resources. I am pleased that the agreement with the Christian People's Party and the Left allows us to continue the main lines of agricultural policy, and that the changes that have been made are not reversed. This ensures the predictability and stability of the industry, and I believe that with this direction the optimism and the faith of the future in the industry will continue.
