ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e4551	The Agriculture Communication is about the future of both the practitioners and industry. Here, a forward trajectory is to be drawn. The background to the Communication is, and must be, changing the world and facing new demands and challenges for agriculture. If we look at developments in a historical perspective, the industry has been subject to a major transformation from the 1950s to the present. The channelling policy that we got at the beginning of the 1950s and the technological developments that took off at the same time changed agriculture forever. In 1950, 844 million hours were spent in agriculture. The operations were versatile, and even small farms had cows. In 1959, the average dairy cows were four cows. In 2015, the average crew was 25.6 cows, and the number of hours was down to 92 million. Technological developments have been formidable during the same period – from hand milking to robotic milking, from horse and cart to tractor, from self-binding and threshing to harvesters, from versatile operations to specialisation, from many hands to few hands, from a soothing life to empty operating buildings, from being a job for many to becoming a part-time job for the few and with substantial income from other work. It may sound like a slowdown, but the conclusion is that hardly anyone wants to return to the time and all the physically heavy work that is behind the 1950s, or at the time when vacation and leisure were definitely an unknown concept for many. The development of agriculture has helped the agricultural population to share in the overall development of wealth that has benefited the general population. The farmers have been good at using new knowledge and technology. Research has played a role with professionals in a necessary symbiosis. Some have gone ahead, and others have followed. This has led us today to a long way in technological development and genetics. In addition, we have healthy animals and efficient production. The fear that major herds will undermine animal welfare has not been activated. Today, new quantum leaps are taking place in technological development. Automation and robotization are speeding. A new concept of precision agriculture has been introduced, where robotics technology allows the use of both pesticides, fertilisers and fuels to be reduced. When this government joined, they wanted to change their course in relation to what had been in force for some years – not for the sake of change, but because the world around us had changed. The reality we live in has changed. One cannot solve the challenges of the future by clinging to the past. If the status quo were the target, you wouldn't need an agricultural message. The main objective of agricultural policy shall be to increase food production in order to meet the needs of a growing population. If this is to be successful, it will have to facilitate the good development of the agricultural industry for small and large uses throughout the country. But then you have to give those who are willing to bet, the law and the ability to do just that. The financial prerequisites for investing and operating must be good enough to enable an operator to renew and deploy new technology. With such a sharp reduction in the number of hours spent in agriculture as we have seen throughout the post-war period, the role of agriculture in rural policy will have to be seen in a broader context than in the past. There is a need for a wider range of jobs in rural areas — not everyone can be full-time farmers. If the settlement is to be maintained, there must be a range of other attractive jobs in a reasonable vicinity that can meet the needs of the inhabitants. Agricultural policy must be designed to strengthen the competitiveness of Norwegian food producers. Norwegian agriculture faces competition for labour and capital — from other industries and from non-resident food producers. It is therefore essential that agricultural policy enables farmers to meet these developments. Norway is a grassland where the agricultural area is best suited for the production of roughage. Only 3 pst. of Norway's area of 325 million deer is arable and only one third of this is suitable for cereal production. With so little cultivated land, it is clear that the land area becomes extremely important. 138 million plots of land are considered useful for grazing. This must also be taken into account in order to understand the high level of conflict in predatory policies. In Norway, the use of pasture land is more important than in most other countries. If there's something we can learn from the past, then it's that the opportunities that lie in the future are going to be put into practice with or without the blessing of politicians. It's always been like that. It provides a basis for optimism.

For the Christian People's Party, it has been important to contribute to the broad majority of the Storting and to continue predictability of the actors in the agricultural industry. I am pleased that today the Stortinge said no to the government's proposal to make several fundamental changes to agricultural policy. Those representatives of the government parties who may be a little disappointed today and who have repeated many times that agriculture has never gone better than it is now, under this government, may be comforted by the continuation of the predictability and the necessary framework conditions that have led to this success. Today's setting has ambitious agricultural targets. We're going to produce more food than today, and we're going to produce food and feed that it's ready to produce in our country. In many areas, there is a potential for increased value creation in the future. It applies within cattle, food grain, fruit and vegetables, and the Christian People's Party believes that more should be invested in these productions. We should both produce more fruit and vegetables and eat more fruit and vegetables. Five a day is a good delivery gel, and if we facilitate and stimulate increased production and increased consumption of fruit and vegetables, society can save billions of dollars over the health budget. But we know that the risk within these productions is high. It is therefore important to focus on research and development in this sector which can contribute to increased competitiveness, growth and sustainable future production of fruit and vegetables. Among fruit producers there is a broad consensus that common stocks and packing plants are essential for quality, product development and increased sales volume. Therefore, targeted arrangements for these stocks and packing centres and the strengthening of these schemes are important for, inter alia, the following: to be able to make adjustments when market imbalances arise. I am pleased that there is an overall committee that also expresses this in the vote, and I am not least pleased with the initiatives that are now being taken in the field of recruitment to the fruit and vegetables sector.

The proposal reads as follows: ‘The legislative decision is not accepted. Note: Under I, the following provision shall be presumed to be amended as follows: Paragraphs 9-11, second paragraph, are replaced by the following: The benefit is graduated for each hour the child is supervised by others, measured against a normal week of work of 37.5 hours, see also Section 9-12, seventh paragraph. Performance can be scaled down to 20 percent. If the child is supervised by more than 80 %, no allowance is available.’

ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e6171	The President proposes that paragraph 1 of Document 8:77 S for 2016-2017 be sent to the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence Policy and paragraph 2 be dealt with in accordance with point (e) of the second paragraph of Article 39 of the Rules of Procedure. The representative, Liv Signe Navarsete, has asked for the floor.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e6178	Point 2 of the proposal for a decision in Document 8:77 S for 2016-2017 states: ‘The Government asks the Government to ensure that the main helicopter base is maintained at Bardufoss, with its operational and technical management, training, training, maintenance, logistics and administration.’; The President's recommendation not to send this proposal for management to the Committee is not justified and therefore difficult to accept. In the setting of the defence long-term plan, the main base of the helicopter is not mentioned in any decision. How, then, can one say that the decision to keep the main base on Bardufoss is contrary to the decision in the long-term plan? The 339-square drone is a pure quadron. The main task of the 339 Quadron is tactical transportation support for the Army and support for special operations. The main helicopter base on Bardufoss and 139 Luftving is an organisational superstructure for all quadrons in Norway as well as those bases that don't have a natural superstructure locally – that is, not the main helicopter base and the 339 squadron. We want to encourage the presidency to do something about how one defines "head base for helicopter" and how one thinks that a main base has to contain a 339 quadrant. This is unknown to me, and it's unknown to defensemen and soldiers in Norway. If last year the bulk of the population actually decided to lay down the main base for helicopters on Bardufoss, it should be explicitly stated. Nevertheless, there is no other way to interpret the president's recommendation than that last year's Stortinget decided to lay down the main base for helicopters on Bardufoss without any decision being taken. On this basis, I would like to request that Document 8:77 S for 2016-2017 be transmitted to the Foreign and Defence Committee in its entirety, and that the Committee be able to discuss and take decisions at both points of decision.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-04-25.ana.ud152e6196	Then it opens for a round of explanations of vote, if anyone wants to take the floor on the matter. The representative Svein Roald Hansen has asked for the floor.
